Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, Doctor. Thank you so much for fitting me in. I really appreciate it. Um, I hope I'm not wasting your time—it's probably just a bad habit I've picked up, really. But... well, I guess what brings me in is that I've been having a little trouble just... staying in my chair when I'm supposed to stay put. 

Like, I'll be in the middle of a client interview—I'm a private investigator, so making people comfortable is really important—or, um, at a tutoring session, or even just sitting with my friends at book club, and I just... sort of pop up. I always tell myself I just need to stretch my legs, you know? But people are starting to notice, and I'm really worried it makes it look like I can't just sit and listen to them. I really don't want to come across as rude or disrespectful, because I do care about what they're saying. Does that make sense? Is that... is that the sort of thing you can help with?

I hope I'm not being too vague, I just don't want to make it sound worse than it actually is.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this restlessness and urge to move around? Has this been going on your whole life, or did it start more recently?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh, definitely not my whole life! That's when I had to move out of my old apartment in Chicago. Moving is always such a hassle, you know, and it throws everything out of order, so I figured maybe the change in routine just made me a little more restless than usual. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, um... well, to be completely honest, I suppose it hasn't *always* been perfectly easy for me to stay seated. I mean, I really don't want to make it sound like I was a bad kid or anything! I was always very polite, I promise. 

But, um, if I think way back... my grade-school report cards did usually say things like... well, that I was bright, but that I had a hard time staying in my seat or waiting my turn. My mother actually still jokes about it—she says I treated every classroom like a crime scene I just *had* to pace through to investigate. It's just a silly joke she makes, though! 

So, I guess I've always been a little bit on the go? But I really try my best to keep it under control now, especially for work. I hope that doesn't make me sound too difficult or disruptive? I've always tried so hard to be good about it.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, um... well, I suppose I might occasionally jump into things a little quickly, but I really don't mean any harm by it! I just get so enthusiastic about my work, you know?

I might just sort of pop in and offer some advice or ask a quick question before they've actually invited me to join in. I just want to be helpful! I just want to make sure I'm answering exactly what you're asking, but I really don't think I have any real trouble concentrating.\n\nDoctor: Beyond the restlessness and trouble sitting still, do you find yourself doing things without thinking them through first, or interrupting people, or making quick decisions you later regret? How often does that happen?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I suppose I might occasionally jump into things a little quickly, but I really don't mean any harm by it! I just get so enthusiastic about my work, you know? \n\nLike, sometimes if I'm at the agency and I hear the junior detectives discussing a lead, or even just, you know, a neighbor talking in the hallway about something going on... I might just sort of pop in and offer some advice or ask a quick question before they've actually invited me to join in. I just want to be helpful! But then afterward, I'll think, \"Oh dear, I just barged right into their conversation, didn't I?\" \n\nI really try to catch myself, though. I know it's not the most polite thing to do, and I'd hate for anyone to think I was being rude or nosy. I just get caught up in trying to solve the puzzle, I guess. Is it okay that I get a little overeager like that sometimes? I promise I'm working on being more patient and waiting my turn to speak.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you're working on a case as a private investigator, where you need to review documents or evidence carefully for extended periods, do you find yourself able to maintain focus on that detailed work, or do you struggle to keep your attention on it?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 49 / private_detective_or_investigator / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, reviewing documents? Um, I'm actually quite good at that! I mean, reading through files and evidence is a huge part of being an investigator, so I really make sure I'm doing it properly. I wouldn't be very good at my job if I couldn't handle the paperwork, right?

I don't really struggle with it at all. I mean, sure, if I've been staring at tiny print for hours and hours, I might need to stand up and stretch my legs, or maybe grab a cup of tea just to refresh my eyes. But that's just normal fatigue, isn't it? I always sit right back down and get the job done. I'm very dedicated to making sure I don't miss a single detail for my clients. 

So, yes, I'd say my focus on detailed work is perfectly fine. I hope that's the kind of answer you were looking for? I just want to reassure you that I'm very capable when it comes to the serious parts of my job.

Oh, um... well, I really try to keep it to an absolute minimum! But I suppose if I'm being completely honest, it might happen... oh, maybe a few times a week? I really hope that doesn't make me sound terribly rude! 

It's just like I was mentioning earlier—if I hear the junior detectives discussing a lead, or even just a neighbor talking in the hallway, my investigator instincts just sort of take over. I end up jumping right in with questions or giving advice before I've even been asked. And then, of course, a minute later I realize I've completely barged into someone else's conversation again. 

I always feel so bad about it when it happens! It really is just because I care so much and want to help, not because I'm trying to be overbearing. Does that make sense? I just want to make sure you don't think I'm a nuisance to the people around me!

activities quietly.\n  manifestation: Quiet things that used to help me, like yoga or reading at book club, now turn into me whispering comments, rustling papers, or finding some excuse to move around instead of just being still.\n- key: S014\n  name: Constant_Activity\n  description: Is often 'on the go', acting as if 'driven by a motor'.\n  manifestation: On days when I don't have field work, I still move from unpacking boxes to reorganizing case files to running errands like I'm chasing a lead, and I have a hard time just being off duty.\n- key: S015\n  name: Excessive_Talking\n  description: Often talks excessively.\n  manifestation: I catch myself talking through every angle of a case or a client meeting until I've basically filled all the space, even when the other person is giving me clear signs they're done.\n- key: S018\n  name: Interrupting_Intruding\n  description: Often interrupts or intrudes on others (e.g., butts into conversations, games, or activities).\n  manifestation: If I hear junior detectives discussing a lead or a neighbor talking in the hallway, I jump in with questions or advice before I've been asked, and afterward I realize I barged into someone else's conversation.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: My grade-school report cards always said I was bright but couldn't stay in my seat or wait my turn, and my mother still jokes that I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: It's starting to cost me professionally—I've missed details in investigations, irritated clients, and made junior detectives less willing to debrief with me because they feel steamrolled.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 8 months\n  manifestation: This has been noticeably worse for about 8 months, since I had to move out of my old Chicago apartment.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 49 / private_detective_or_investigator / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S011\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Beyond the restlessness and trouble sitting still, do you find yourself doing things without thinking them through first, or interrupting people, or making quick decisions you later regret? How often does that happen?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I suppose I might occasionally jump into things a little quickly, but I really don't mean any harm by it! I just get so enthusiastic about my work, you know? \n\nLike, sometimes if I'm at the agency and I hear the junior detectives discussing a lead, or even just, you know, a neighbor talking in the hallway about something going on... I might just sort of pop in and offer some advice or ask a quick question before they've actually invited me to join in. I just want to be helpful! But then afterward, I'll think, \"Oh dear, I just barged right into their conversation, didn't I?\" \n\nI really try to catch myself, though. I wouldn't be very good at my job if I couldn't handle the paperwork, right?\n\nI don't really struggle with it at all. I mean, sure, if I've been staring at tiny print for hours and hours, I might need to stand up and stretch my legs, or maybe grab a cup of tea just to refresh my eyes. I'm very dedicated to making sure I don't miss a single detail for my clients. \n\nSo, yes, I'd say my focus on detailed work is perfectly fine. I hope that's the kind of answer you were looking for? I just want to reassure you that I'm very capable when it comes to the serious parts of my job.\n\nDoctor: When you interrupt people in conversations or jump into situations quickly, how often would you say that happens—is it something that occurs several times a week, or is it more occasional?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I really try to keep it to an absolute minimum! But I suppose if I'm being completely honest, it might happen... oh, maybe a few times a week? I really hope that doesn't make me sound terribly rude! \n\nIt's just like I was mentioning earlier—if I hear the junior detectives discussing a lead, or even just a neighbor talking in the hallway, my investigator instincts just sort of take over. I end up jumping right in with questions or giving advice before I've even been asked. And then, of course, a minute later I realize I've completely barged into someone else's conversation again. \n\nI always feel so bad about it when it happens! It really is just because I care so much and want to help, not because I'm trying to be overbearing. Does that make sense? I just want to make sure you don't think I'm a nuisance to the people around me!\n\n[Last Question]\nYou mentioned your grade-school report cards noted difficulty staying in your seat and waiting your turn—did you also have trouble with interrupting people or jumping into things quickly back then, or is that interrupting habit something that developed more recently?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I've always tried so hard to be good about it.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your attention is usually okay, but I'd like to ask more specifically: Do you find yourself getting distracted or having trouble concentrating even when you're trying hard to focus, like during those client interviews or other situations where you need to pay close attention?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, my focus is actually quite good! I really pride myself on being a good listener, especially with my clients. I mean, they're paying me to pay attention to the details of their cases, so I make absolutely sure I'm locked in. \n\nI don't really get distracted... at least, not in a way that messes up my work, I promise. Sometimes my mind might wander just a tiny bit if a meeting drags on for hours and hours, but I think that happens to everyone, right? It's not like I'm zoning out or missing important information. I'm always very careful to take good notes and stay on track. \n\nDoes that sound normal to you? I just want to make sure I'm answering exactly what you're asking, but I really don't think I have any real trouble concentrating.\n\nDoctor: Beyond the restlessness and trouble sitting still, do you find yourself doing things without thinking them through first, or interrupting people, or making quick decisions you later regret? How often does that happen?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I suppose I might occasionally jump into things a little quickly, but I really don't mean any harm by it! I just get so enthusiastic about my work, you know? \n\nLike, sometimes if I'm at the agency and I hear the junior detectives discussing a lead, or even just, you know, a neighbor talking in the hallway about something going on... I might just sort of pop in and offer some advice or ask a quick question before they've actually invited me to join in. I just want to be helpful! But then afterward, I'll think, \"Oh dear, I just barged right into their conversation, didn't I?\" \n\nI really try to catch myself, though. I know it's not the most polite thing to do, and I'd hate for anyone to think I was being rude or nosy. I just get caught up in trying to solve the puzzle, I guess. Is it okay that I get a little overeager like that sometimes? I promise I'm working on being more patient and waiting my turn to speak.\n\nDoctor: When you're working on a case as a private investigator, where you need to review documents or evidence carefully for extended periods, do you find yourself able to maintain focus on that detailed work, or do you struggle to keep your attention on it?\n\nPatient: Oh, reviewing documents? Um, I'm actually quite good at that! I mean, reading through files and evidence is a huge part of being an investigator, so I really make sure I'm doing it properly. I wouldn't be very good at my job if I couldn't handle the paperwork, right?\n\nI don't really struggle with it at all. I mean, sure, if I've been staring at tiny print for hours and hours, I might need to stand up and stretch my legs, or maybe grab a cup of tea just to refresh my eyes. But that's just normal fatigue, isn't it? I always sit right back down and get the job done. I'm very dedicated to making sure I don't miss a single detail for my clients. \n\nSo, yes, I'd say my focus on detailed work is perfectly fine. I hope that's the kind of answer you were looking for? I just want to reassure you that I'm very capable when it comes to the serious parts of my job.\n\nDoctor: When you interrupt people in conversations or jump into situations quickly, how often would you say that happens—is it something that occurs several times a week, or is it more occasional?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I really try to keep it to an absolute minimum! Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, um... well, no, I don't think they ever used the word "hyperactive." I really don't! That sounds so... so extreme, doesn't it? 

I mean, my mother might have said I was a handful sometimes, or that I had a lot of spirit, but she always said it with a smile. And my teachers, like I said, they just noted that I was eager. I don't think anyone ever felt I had *too* much energy compared to the other children. I was just... lively! 

I really tried my best to be a good girl and not be too much for anyone to handle. I hope it doesn't seem like I was a burden on my parents or teachers. Having a lot of spirit is a good thing for a little girl, isn't it?

It's not like there's something wrong with my legs or anything. It's probably more just... a little bit of extra energy, maybe? \n\nIt just feels like my body is ready to move on to the next thing, even if my brain knows we need to stay seated for the client. It's really not a big, dramatic urge, just a tiny bit of restlessness, I suppose. Does that sound like what you were asking? I hope I'm not being too vague, I just don't want to make it sound worse than it actually is.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this restlessness and urge to move around? Has this been going on your whole life, or did it start more recently?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh, definitely not my whole life! I mean, I've always been a little energetic, I suppose, but nothing disruptive, I promise. \n\nIt's really just been... well, it's been noticeably worse for about eight months now? That's when I had to move out of my old apartment in Chicago. Moving is always such a hassle, you know, and it throws everything out of order, so I figured maybe the change in routine just made me a little more restless than usual. So it's just a recent thing, really, not some lifelong problem. \n\nI hope that's a helpful timeline for you? Does it make sense that a big move might trigger a little extra fidgeting like that?\n\nDoctor: Before you moved eight months ago, would you say you were able to sit still comfortably during work meetings or social situations, or have you always found it somewhat difficult to stay seated even back then?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, to be completely honest, I suppose it hasn't *always* been perfectly easy for me to stay seated. I mean, I really don't want to make it sound like I was a bad kid or anything! I was always very polite, I promise. \n\nBut, um, if I think way back... my grade-school report cards did usually say things like... well, that I was bright, but that I had a hard time staying in my seat or waiting my turn. My mother actually still jokes about it—she says I treated every classroom like a crime scene I just *had* to pace through to investigate. It's just a silly joke she makes, though! \n\nSo, I guess I've always been a little bit on the go? But I really try my best to keep it under control now, especially for work. I hope that doesn't make me sound too difficult or disruptive? I've always tried so hard to be good about it.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your attention is usually okay, but I'd like to ask more specifically: Do you find yourself getting distracted or having trouble concentrating even when you're trying hard to focus, like during those client interviews or other situations where you need to pay close attention?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, my focus is actually quite good! I really pride myself on being a good listener, especially with my clients. I mean, they're paying me to pay attention to the details of their cases, so I make absolutely sure I'm locked in. \n\nI don't really get distracted... at least, not in a way that messes up my work, I promise. Sometimes my mind might wander just a tiny bit if a meeting drags on for hours and hours, but I think that happens to everyone, right? It's not like I'm zoning out or missing important information. I'm always very careful to take good notes and stay on track. \n\nDoes that sound normal to you? How often does that happen?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I suppose I might occasionally jump into things a little quickly, but I really don't mean any harm by it! I just get so enthusiastic about my work, you know? \n\nLike, sometimes if I'm at the agency and I hear the junior detectives discussing a lead, or even just, you know, a neighbor talking in the hallway about something going on... I might just sort of pop in and offer some advice or ask a quick question before they've actually invited me to join in. I just want to be helpful! But then afterward, I'll think, \"Oh dear, I just barged right into their conversation, didn't I?\" \n\nI really try to catch myself, though. I know it's not the most polite thing to do, and I'd hate for anyone to think I was being rude or nosy. I just get caught up in trying to solve the puzzle, I guess. Is it okay that I get a little overeager like that sometimes? I promise I'm working on being more patient and waiting my turn to speak.\n\nDoctor: When you're working on a case as a private investigator, where you need to review documents or evidence carefully for extended periods, do you find yourself able to maintain focus on that detailed work, or do you struggle to keep your attention on it?\n\nPatient: Oh, reviewing documents? Um, I'm actually quite good at that! I mean, reading through files and evidence is a huge part of being an investigator, so I really make sure I'm doing it properly. I wouldn't be very good at my job if I couldn't handle the paperwork, right?\n\nI don't really struggle with it at all. I mean, sure, if I've been staring at tiny print for hours and hours, I might need to stand up and stretch my legs, or maybe grab a cup of tea just to refresh my eyes. But that's just normal fatigue, isn't it? I always sit right back down and get the job done. I'm very dedicated to making sure I don't miss a single detail for my clients. \n\nSo, yes, I'd say my focus on detailed work is perfectly fine. I hope that's the kind of answer you were looking for? I just want to reassure you that I'm very capable when it comes to the serious parts of my job.\n\nDoctor: When you interrupt people in conversations or jump into situations quickly, how often would you say that happens—is it something that occurs several times a week, or is it more occasional?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I really try to keep it to an absolute minimum! But I suppose if I'm being completely honest, it might happen... oh, maybe a few times a week? I really hope that doesn't make me sound terribly rude! \n\nIt's just like I was mentioning earlier—if I hear the junior detectives discussing a lead, or even just a neighbor talking in the hallway, my investigator instincts just sort of take over. I end up jumping right in with questions or giving advice before I've even been asked. It really is just because I care so much and want to help, not because I'm trying to be overbearing. Does that make sense? I just want to make sure you don't think I'm a nuisance to the people around me!\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your grade-school report cards noted difficulty staying in your seat and waiting your turn—did you also have trouble with interrupting people or jumping into things quickly back then, or is that interrupting habit something that developed more recently?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, yes, I suppose it goes back quite a ways. I really hope that doesn't make me sound like I was a terrible child! I promise I was always very well-behaved and polite overall. \n\nBut, yes, my grade-school report cards... well, the teachers always wrote that I was very bright! They made sure to say that. But they did also mention that I had a bit of trouble staying in my seat, and, um, waiting my turn to speak. I guess I've always been a bit eager to jump in. My mother actually still jokes about it—she says I treated every classroom like a crime scene that I just had to pace through and investigate immediately. \n\nI just had so much energy and curiosity, you know? I was just so eager to learn and participate. I really hope that doesn't sound like I was a disruptive student. I always meant well, even back then. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, um... well... I suppose if I really look back and compare myself to the very quiet children, maybe I was a *little* more active. 

I mean, if you looked at my grade-school report cards, they always made a point to say that I was very bright! Which is a wonderful thing, right? I was a very smart little girl. But... well, they did usually add that I couldn't always stay in my seat, or that I had a hard time waiting my turn to speak. I just got so eager to participate, you know? 

My mother actually still teases me about it sometimes. Because of my work now, she jokes that even back then, I treated every classroom like a crime scene that I just *had* to pace through and investigate. 

She just means it affectionately, though! I wasn't trying to be restless or difficult, I promise. I just had a very curious mind and wanted to be involved in everything. That's a positive trait for a child to have, wouldn't you agree? I really hope it doesn't make me sound like I was a troublemaker.

I mean, I've always been a little energetic, I suppose, but nothing disruptive, I promise. \n\nIt's really just been... well, it's been noticeably worse for about eight months now? That's when I had to move out of my old apartment in Chicago. Moving is always such a hassle, you know, and it throws everything out of order, so I figured maybe the change in routine just made me a little more restless than usual. So it's just a recent thing, really, not some lifelong problem. \n\nI hope that's a helpful timeline for you? Does it make sense that a big move might trigger a little extra fidgeting like that?\n\nDoctor: Before you moved eight months ago, would you say you were able to sit still comfortably during work meetings or social situations, or have you always found it somewhat difficult to stay seated even back then?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, to be completely honest, I suppose it hasn't *always* been perfectly easy for me to stay seated. I mean, I really don't want to make it sound like I was a bad kid or anything! I was always very polite, I promise. \n\nBut, um, if I think way back... my grade-school report cards did usually say things like... well, that I was bright, but that I had a hard time staying in my seat or waiting my turn. My mother actually still jokes about it—she says I treated every classroom like a crime scene I just *had* to pace through to investigate. It's just a silly joke she makes, though! \n\nSo, I guess I've always been a little bit on the go? But I really try my best to keep it under control now, especially for work. I hope that doesn't make me sound too difficult or disruptive? I've always tried so hard to be good about it.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your attention is usually okay, but I'd like to ask more specifically: Do you find yourself getting distracted or having trouble concentrating even when you're trying hard to focus, like during those client interviews or other situations where you need to pay close attention?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, my focus is actually quite good! I really pride myself on being a good listener, especially with my clients. I mean, they're paying me to pay attention to the details of their cases, so I make absolutely sure I'm locked in. \n\nI don't really get distracted... at least, not in a way that messes up my work, I promise. Sometimes my mind might wander just a tiny bit if a meeting drags on for hours and hours, but I think that happens to everyone, right? It's not like I'm zoning out or missing important information. I'm always very careful to take good notes and stay on track. \n\nDoes that sound normal to you? I just want to make sure I'm answering exactly what you're asking, but I really don't think I have any real trouble concentrating.\n\nDoctor: Beyond the restlessness and trouble sitting still, do you find yourself doing things without thinking them through first, or interrupting people, or making quick decisions you later regret? How often does that happen?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I suppose I might occasionally jump into things a little quickly, but I really don't mean any harm by it! I just get so enthusiastic about my work, you know? \n\nLike, sometimes if I'm at the agency and I hear the junior detectives discussing a lead, or even just, you know, a neighbor talking in the hallway about something going on... I might just sort of pop in and offer some advice or ask a quick question before they've actually invited me to join in. I just want to be helpful! But then afterward, I'll think, \"Oh dear, I just barged right into their conversation, didn't I?\" \n\nI really try to catch myself, though. I know it's not the most polite thing to do, and I'd hate for anyone to think I was being rude or nosy. I just get caught up in trying to solve the puzzle, I guess. Is it okay that I get a little overeager like that sometimes? I promise I'm working on being more patient and waiting my turn to speak.\n\nDoctor: When you're working on a case as a private investigator, where you need to review documents or evidence carefully for extended periods, do you find yourself able to maintain focus on that detailed work, or do you struggle to keep your attention on it?\n\nPatient: Oh, reviewing documents? Um, I'm actually quite good at that! I mean, reading through files and evidence is a huge part of being an investigator, so I really make sure I'm doing it properly. I wouldn't be very good at my job if I couldn't handle the paperwork, right?\n\nI don't really struggle with it at all. I mean, sure, if I've been staring at tiny print for hours and hours, I might need to stand up and stretch my legs, or maybe grab a cup of tea just to refresh my eyes. But that's just normal fatigue, isn't it? I always sit right back down and get the job done. I'm very dedicated to making sure I don't miss a single detail for my clients. \n\nSo, yes, I'd say my focus on detailed work is perfectly fine. I hope that's the kind of answer you were looking for? I just want to reassure you that I'm very capable when it comes to the serious parts of my job.\n\nDoctor: When you interrupt people in conversations or jump into situations quickly, how often would you say that happens—is it something that occurs several times a week, or is it more occasional?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I really try to keep it to an absolute minimum! But I suppose if I'm being completely honest, it might happen... oh, maybe a few times a week? I really hope that doesn't make me sound terribly rude! \n\nIt's just like I was mentioning earlier—if I hear the junior detectives discussing a lead, or even just a neighbor talking in the hallway, my investigator instincts just sort of take over. I end up jumping right in with questions or giving advice before I've even been asked. And then, of course, a minute later I realize I've completely barged into someone else's conversation again. \n\nI always feel so bad about it when it happens! It really is just because I care so much and want to help, not because I'm trying to be overbearing. Does that make sense? I just want to make sure you don't think I'm a nuisance to the people around me!\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your grade-school report cards noted difficulty staying in your seat and waiting your turn—did you also have trouble with interrupting people or jumping into things quickly back then, or is that interrupting habit something that developed more recently?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, yes, I suppose it goes back quite a ways. I really hope that doesn't make me sound like I was a terrible child! I promise I was always very well-behaved and polite overall. \n\nBut, yes, my grade-school report cards... well, the teachers always wrote that I was very bright! They made sure to say that. But they did also mention that I had a bit of trouble staying in my seat, and, um, waiting my turn to speak. I guess I've always been a bit eager to jump in. My mother actually still jokes about it—she says I treated every classroom like a crime scene that I just had to pace through and investigate immediately. \n\nI just had so much energy and curiosity, you know? I was just so eager to learn and participate. I really hope that doesn't sound like I was a disruptive student. I always meant well, even back then. Do you think it's okay that I was like that as a little girl?\n\nDoctor: When you were in grade school, beyond having trouble staying in your seat and waiting your turn to speak, would you say you were also very physically active—like, always running around, fidgeting a lot, or having a hard time playing quietly with other children?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I wouldn't say I was *always* running around or anything like that. I mean, I definitely liked to play and be active, just like any normal child, right? \n\nI don't think I had a hard time playing quietly with the other children. I always tried very hard to be a good friend and play nicely. If we were playing a quiet game, I'd certainly do my best to join in properly. \n\nI suppose maybe I fidgeted a little bit? Just, you know, tapping my foot or playing with my pencil if I was really excited about a lesson. But I don't think I was overly hyper or disruptive. I really hope I'm not painting a picture of a little girl who was just bouncing off the walls and causing trouble for everyone! I was just a very enthusiastic child. Does that sound fairly normal to you?\n\nDoctor: When you were in grade school, did your teachers or parents ever describe you as hyperactive, or say that you had too much energy compared to other kids your age?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, no, I don't think they ever used the word \"hyperactive.\" I really don't! That sounds so... so extreme, doesn't it? \n\nI mean, my mother might have said I was a handful sometimes, or that I had a lot of spirit, but she always said it with a smile. And my teachers, like I said, they just noted that I was eager. I don't think anyone ever felt I had *too* much energy compared to the other children. I was just... lively! \n\nI really tried my best to be a good girl and not be too much for anyone to handle. I hope it doesn't seem like I was a burden on my parents or teachers. Having a lot of spirit is a good thing for a little girl, isn't it?\n\nDoctor: When you were in grade school, did you have trouble sitting still during meals, or did you find yourself getting up and moving around a lot during times when you were expected to stay seated—like at dinner with your family or during class?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, I really don't think so. Dinner time was always very important in our house, so I made sure to sit nicely with my parents. I mean, maybe I would ask to be excused a little early if I had finished all my vegetables, but I certainly wasn't just getting up and wandering around while everyone else was still eating. That would have been terribly rude, wouldn't it? I always wanted to have good table manners. \n\nAnd in class... well, like I said before, I might have tapped my pencil, but I knew I was supposed to stay at my desk. I respected my teachers too much to just get up and walk around during a lesson. I really tried my hardest to be polite and follow the rules. That's what a good student is supposed to do, right? Just sit and listen until it's time for recess?\n\nDoctor: When you think back to grade school, compared to the other kids in your class, would you say you were moving around, fidgeting, or physically active about the same amount as they were, or would you say you were noticeably more active and restless than most of them?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well... I suppose if I really look back and compare myself to the very quiet children, maybe I was a *little* more active. \n\nI mean, if you looked at my grade-school report cards, they always made a point to say that I was very bright! Which is a wonderful thing, right? I was a very smart little girl. But... well, they did usually add that I couldn't always stay in my seat, or that I had a hard time waiting my turn to speak. I just got so eager to participate, you know? \n\nMy mother actually still teases me about it sometimes. Because of my work now, she jokes that even back then, I treated every classroom like a crime scene that I just *had* to pace through and investigate. \n\nShe just means it affectionately, though! I wasn't trying to be restless or difficult, I promise. I just had a very curious mind and wanted to be involved in everything. That's a positive trait for a child to have, wouldn't you agree? I really hope it doesn't make me sound like I was a troublemaker.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },
